Medici">Giulio di Giuliano de' Medici</a></th><td class="infobox-data">21 December 1517</td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label"><a href="/wiki/Ferdinando_Ponzetti" title="Ferdinando Ponzetti">Ferdinando Ponzetti</a></th><td class="infobox-data">21 December 1517</td></tr></tbody></table></td></tr></tbody></table> 
<p><b>Pope Leo X</b> (<a href="/wiki/Italian_language" title="Italian language">Italian</a>: <i lang="it">Leone X</i>; born <b>Giovanni di Lorenzo de' Medici</b>, 11 December 1475&#160;&#8211;&#32;1 December 1521) was head of the <a href="/wiki/Catholic_Church" title="Catholic Church">Catholic Church</a> and ruler of the <a href="/wiki/Papal_States" title="Papal States">Papal States</a> from 9 March 1513 to his death, in December 1521.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELöffler1910_2-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELöffler1910-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Born into the prominent political and banking <a href="/wiki/Medici_family" class="mw-redirect" title="Medici family">Medici family</a> of <a href="/wiki/Republic_of_Florence" title="Republic of Florence">Florence</a>, Giovanni was the second son of <a href="/wiki/Lorenzo_de%27_Medici" title="Lorenzo de&#39; Medici">Lorenzo de' Medici</a>, ruler of the <a href="/wiki/Florentine_Republic" class="mw-redirect" title="Florentine Republic">Florentine Republic</a>, and was elevated to the <a href="/wiki/Cardinal_(Catholicism)" class="mw-redirect" title="Cardinal (Catholicism)">cardinalate</a> in 1489. Following the death of <a href="/wiki/Pope_Julius_II" title="Pope Julius II">Pope Julius II</a>, Giovanni was elected pope after securing the backing of the younger members of the <a href="/wiki/College_of_Cardinals" title="College of Cardinals">Sacred College</a>. Early on in his rule he oversaw the closing sessions of the <a href="/wiki/Fifth_Council_of_the_Lateran" title="Fifth Council of the Lateran">Fifth Council of the Lateran</a>, but struggled to implement the reforms agreed. In 1517 he led a costly <a href="/wiki/War_of_Urbino" title="War of Urbino">war</a> that succeeded in securing his nephew <a href="/wiki/Lorenzo_de%27_Medici,_Duke_of_Urbino" title="Lorenzo de&#39; Medici, Duke of Urbino">Lorenzo di Piero de' Medici</a> as Duke of <a href="/wiki/Urbino" title="Urbino">Urbino</a>, but reduced papal finances.
</p><p>In Protestant circles, Leo is associated with granting <a href="/wiki/Indulgences" class="mw-redirect" title="Indulgences">indulgences</a> for those who donated to reconstruct <a href="/wiki/St._Peter%27s_Basilica" title="St. Peter&#39;s Basilica">St. Peter's Basilica</a>, a practice that was soon challenged by <a href="/wiki/Martin_Luther" title="Martin Luther">Martin Luther</a>'s <i><a href="/wiki/95_Theses" class="mw-redirect" title="95 Theses">95 Theses</a></i>. Leo rejected the <a href="/wiki/Protestant_Reformation" class="mw-redirect" title="Protestant Reformation">Protestant Reformation</a>, and his <a href="/wiki/Papal_bull" title="Papal bull">Papal bull</a> of 1520, <i><a href="/wiki/Exsurge_Domine" title="Exsurge Domine">Exsurge Domine</a></i>, condemned Luther's condemnatory stance, rendering ongoing communication difficult.
</p><p>He borrowed and spent money without circumspection and was a significant patron of the arts. Under his reign, progress was made on the rebuilding of St. Peter's Basilica and artists such as <a href="/wiki/Raphael" title="Raphael">Raphael</a> decorated the <a href="/wiki/Raphael_Rooms" title="Raphael Rooms">Vatican rooms</a>. Leo also reorganised the <a href="/wiki/Sapienza_University_of_Rome" title="Sapienza University of Rome">Roman University</a>, and promoted the study of literature, poetry and antiquities. He died in 1521 and is buried in <a href="/wiki/Santa_Maria_sopra_Minerva" title="Santa Maria sopra Minerva">Santa Maria sopra Minerva</a>, Rome. He was the last pope not to have been in <a href="/wiki/Priesthood_in_the_Catholic_Church" title="Priesthood in the Catholic Church">priestly orders</a> at the time of his election to the papacy.
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_life">Early life</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Leo_X&amp;action=edit&amp;section=1" title="Edit section: Early life">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Santa_Maria_in_Domnica_-_esterno.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/65/Santa_Maria_in_Domnica_-_esterno.jpg/200px-Santa_Maria_in_Domnica_-_esterno.jpg" decoding="async" width="200" height="185" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/65/Santa_Maria_in_Domnica_-_esterno.jpg/300px-Santa_Maria_in_Domnica_-_esterno.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/65/Santa_Maria_in_Domnica_-_esterno.jpg/400px-Santa_Maria_in_Domnica_-_esterno.jpg 2x" data-file-width="6767" data-file-height="6276" /></a><figcaption>Santa Maria in Domnica</figcaption></figure>
<p>Giovanni di Lorenzo de' Medici was born on 11 December 1475 in the <a href="/wiki/Republic_of_Florence" title="Republic of Florence">Republic of Florence</a>, the second son of <a href="/wiki/Lorenzo_the_Magnificent" class="mw-redirect" title="Lorenzo the Magnificent">Lorenzo the Magnificent</a>, head of the <a href="/wiki/Florentine_Republic" class="mw-redirect" title="Florentine Republic">Florentine Republic</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Clarice_Orsini" title="Clarice Orsini">Clarice Orsini</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELöffler1910_2-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELöffler1910-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup> From an early age Giovanni was destined for an ecclesiastical career. He received the <a href="/wiki/Tonsure" title="Tonsure">tonsure</a> at the age of seven and was soon granted rich <a href="/wiki/Benefice" title="Benefice">benefices</a> and preferments.
</p><p>His father, <a href="/wiki/Lorenzo_de%27_Medici" title="Lorenzo de&#39; Medici">Lorenzo de' Medici</a>, was worried about his character early on and wrote a letter to Giovanni to warn him to avoid vice and luxury at the beginning of his ecclesiastical career. Here is a notable excerpt: "There is one rule which I would recommend to your attention in preference to all others. Rise early in the morning. This will not only contribute to your health, but will enable you to arrange and expedite the business of the day; and as there are various duties incident to".<sup id="cite_ref-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Cardinal">Cardinal</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Leo_X&amp;action=edit&amp;section=2" title="Edit section: Cardinal">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>His father prevailed on his relative <a href="/wiki/Pope_Innocent_VIII" title="Pope Innocent VIII">Pope Innocent VIII</a> to name him <a href="/wiki/Cardinal_(Catholic_Church)" title="Cardinal (Catholic Church)">cardinal</a> of <a href="/wiki/Santa_Maria_in_Domnica" title="Santa Maria in Domnica">Santa Maria in Domnica</a> on 9 March 1489 when he was age 13,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWilliams199871_4-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilliams199871-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> although he was not allowed to wear the insignia or share in the deliberations of the college until three years later. Meanwhile, he received an education at Lorenzo's <a href="/wiki/Humanism" title="Humanism">humanistic</a> <a href="/wiki/Court_(royal)" class="mw-redirect" title="Court (royal)">court</a> under such men as <a href="/wiki/Angelo_Poliziano" class="mw-redirect" title="Angelo Poliziano">Angelo Poliziano</a>, <a href="/wiki/Pico_della_Mirandola" class="mw-redirect" title="Pico della Mirandola">Pico della Mirandola</a>, <a href="/wiki/Marsilio_Ficino" title="Marsilio Ficino">Marsilio Ficino</a> and <a href="/wiki/Bernardo_Dovizio_Bibbiena" class="mw-redirect" title="Bernardo Dovizio Bibbiena">Bernardo Dovizio Bibbiena</a>. From 1489 to 1491 he studied <a href="/wiki/Theology" title="Theology">theology</a> and <a href="/wiki/Canon_law" title="Canon law">canon law</a> at <a href="/wiki/Pisa" title="Pisa">Pisa</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELöffler1910_2-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELöffler1910-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On 23 March 1492, he was formally admitted into the <a href="/wiki/College_of_Cardinals" title="College of Cardinals">Sacred College of Cardinals</a> and took up his residence at <a href="/wiki/Rome" title="Rome">Rome</a>, receiving a letter of advice from his father. The death of Lorenzo on the following 8 April temporarily recalled the 16-year-old Giovanni to Florence. He returned to Rome to participate in the <a href="/wiki/Conclave_of_1492" class="mw-redirect" title="Conclave of 1492">conclave of 1492</a> which followed the death of Innocent VIII, and unsuccessfully opposed the election of <a href="/wiki/Rodrigo_Borgia" class="mw-redirect" title="Rodrigo Borgia">Cardinal Borgia</a> (elected as Pope Alexander VI).
</p><p>He subsequently made his home with his elder brother <a href="/wiki/Piero_di_Lorenzo_de%27_Medici" class="mw-redirect" title="Piero di Lorenzo de&#39; Medici">Piero</a> in Florence throughout the agitation of <a href="/wiki/Girolamo_Savonarola" title="Girolamo Savonarola">Savonarola</a> and the invasion of <a href="/wiki/Charles_VIII_of_France" title="Charles VIII of France">Charles VIII of France</a>, until the uprising of the Florentines and the expulsion of the Medici in November 1494. While Piero found refuge at <a href="/wiki/Venice" title="Venice">Venice</a> and <a href="/wiki/Urbino" title="Urbino">Urbino</a>, Giovanni traveled in <a href="/wiki/Germany" title="Germany">Germany</a>, in the <a href="/wiki/Netherlands" title="Netherlands">Netherlands</a>, and in France.<sup id="cite_ref-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In May 1500, he returned to Rome, where he was received with outward cordiality by <a href="/wiki/Pope_Alexander_VI" title="Pope Alexander VI">Pope Alexander VI</a>, and where he lived for several years immersed in art and literature. In 1503 he welcomed the accession of <a href="/wiki/Pope_Julius_II" title="Pope Julius II">Pope Julius II</a> to the pontificate; the death of Piero de' Medici in the same year made Giovanni head of his family. On 1 October 1511, he was appointed <a href="/wiki/Papal_legate" title="Papal legate">papal legate</a> of <a href="/wiki/Bologna" title="Bologna">Bologna</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Romagna" title="Romagna">Romagna</a>, and when the Florentine republic declared in favour of the schismatic Pisans, Julius II sent Giovanni (as legate) with the papal army venturing against the French. The French won a major battle and captured Giovanni.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESetton1984118_6-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESetton1984118-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> This and other attempts to regain political control of Florence were frustrated until a bloodless revolution permitted the return of the Medici. Giovanni's younger brother <a href="/wiki/Giuliano_de%27_Medici,_Duke_of_Nemours" title="Giuliano de&#39; Medici, Duke of Nemours">Giuliano</a> was placed at the head of the republic,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPhillips-Court201190_7-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPhillips-Court201190-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup> but Giovanni managed the government.
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Pope">Pope</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Leo_X&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section: Pope">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Papal_election">Papal election</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Leo_X&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section: Papal election">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1033289096">.mw-parser-output .hatnote{font-style:italic}.mw-parser-output div.hatnote{padding-left:1.6em;margin-bottom:0.5em}.mw-parser-output .hatnote i{font-style:normal}.mw-parser-output .hatnote+link+.hatnote{margin-top:-0.5em}</style><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Papal_conclave,_1513" class="mw-redirect" title="Papal conclave, 1513">Papal conclave, 1513</a></div>
<p>Giovanni was <a href="/wiki/Papal_conclave,_1513" class="mw-redirect" title="Papal conclave, 1513">elected pope</a> on 9 March 1513, and this was proclaimed two days later.<sup id="cite_ref-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup> The absence of the French cardinals effectively reduced the election to a contest between Giovanni (who had the support of the younger and noble members of the college) and <a href="/wiki/Raffaele_Riario" title="Raffaele Riario">Raffaele Riario</a> (who had the support of the older group). On 15 March 1513, he was ordained priest, and consecrated as bishop on 17 March. He was crowned Pope on 19 March 1513 at the age of 37. He was the last non-priest to be elected pope.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELöffler1910_2-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELöffler1910-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Portrait_of_Pope_Leo_X_and_his_cousins,_cardinals_Giulio_de%27_Medici_and_Luigi_de%27_Rossi_(by_Raphael).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5a/Portrait_of_Pope_Leo_X_and_his_cousins%2C_cardinals_Giulio_de%27_Medici_and_Luigi_de%27_Rossi_%28by_Raphael%29.jpg/270px-Portrait_of_Pope_Leo_X_and_his_cousins%2C_cardinals_Giulio_de%27_Medici_and_Luigi_de%27_Rossi_%28by_Raphael%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="270" height="357" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5a/Portrait_of_Pope_Leo_X_and_his_cousins%2C_cardinals_Giulio_de%27_Medici_and_Luigi_de%27_Rossi_%28by_Raphael%29.jpg/405px-Portrait_of_Pope_Leo_X_and_his_cousins%2C_cardinals_Giulio_de%27_Medici_and_Luigi_de%27_Rossi_%28by_Raphael%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5a/Portrait_of_Pope_Leo_X_and_his_cousins%2C_cardinals_Giulio_de%27_Medici_and_Luigi_de%27_Rossi_%28by_Raphael%29.jpg/540px-Portrait_of_Pope_Leo_X_and_his_cousins%2C_cardinals_Giulio_de%27_Medici_and_Luigi_de%27_Rossi_%28by_Raphael%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2538" data-file-height="3360" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Raphael" title="Raphael">Raphael</a>'s <i><a href="/wiki/Portrait_of_Leo_X_(Raphael)" title="Portrait of Leo X (Raphael)">Portrait of Leo X</a></i> with cardinals <a href="/wiki/Pope_Clement_VII" title="Pope Clement VII">Giulio de' Medici</a> (later <a href="/wiki/Clement_VII" class="mw-redirect" title="Clement VII">Pope Clement VII</a>) and <a href="/wiki/Luigi_de%27_Rossi" title="Luigi de&#39; Rossi">Luigi de' Rossi</a>, his first cousins, (<a href="/wiki/Uffizi" title="Uffizi">Uffizi gallery</a>, Florence)<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMinnichRaphael20031005–1052_9-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMinnichRaphael20031005–1052-9">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup></figcaption></figure>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="War_of_Urbino">War of Urbino</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Leo_X&amp;action=edit&amp;section=5" title="Edit section: War of Urbino">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/War_of_Urbino" title="War of Urbino">War of Urbino</a></div>
<p>Leo had intended his younger brother Giuliano and his nephew <a href="/wiki/Lorenzo_II_de%27_Medici" class="mw-redirect" title="Lorenzo II de&#39; Medici">Lorenzo</a> for brilliant secular careers. He had named them Roman <a href="/wiki/Patrician_(post-Roman_Europe)" title="Patrician (post-Roman Europe)">patricians</a>; the latter he had placed in charge of Florence; the former, for whom he planned to carve out a kingdom in central Italy of Parma, Piacenza, <a href="/wiki/Ferrara" title="Ferrara">Ferrara</a> and Urbino, he had taken with himself to Rome and married to Filiberta of Savoy.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETomas201713_10-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETomas201713-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The death of Giuliano in March 1516, however, caused the pope to transfer his ambitions to Lorenzo. At the very time (December 1516) that peace between France, Spain, Venice and the Empire seemed to give some promise of a <a href="/wiki/Christendom" title="Christendom">Christendom</a> united against the Turks, Leo obtained 150,000 ducats towards the expenses of the expedition from <a href="/wiki/Henry_VIII_of_England" class="mw-redirect" title="Henry VIII of England">Henry VIII of England</a>, in return for which he entered the imperial league of Spain and England against France.<sup id="cite_ref-Chisholm1911_11-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Chisholm1911-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The war lasted from February to September 1517 and ended with the expulsion of the duke and the triumph of Lorenzo; but it revived the policy of Alexander VI, increased <a href="/wiki/Brigandage" title="Brigandage">brigandage</a> and anarchy in the <a href="/wiki/Papal_States" title="Papal States">Papal States</a>, hindered the preparations for a crusade and wrecked the papal finances. <a href="/wiki/Francesco_Guicciardini" title="Francesco Guicciardini">Francesco Guicciardini</a> reckoned the cost of the war to Leo at the sum of 800,000 ducats. Ultimately, however, Lorenzo was confirmed as the new duke of Urbino.<sup id="cite_ref-Chisholm1911_11-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Chisholm1911-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Plans_for_a_Crusade">Plans for a Crusade</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Leo_X&amp;action=edit&amp;section=6" title="Edit section: Plans for a Crusade">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>The war of Urbino was further marked by a crisis in the relations between the pope and the cardinals. The sacred college had allegedly grown very worldly and troublesome since the time of <a href="/wiki/Sixtus_IV" class="mw-redirect" title="Sixtus IV">Sixtus IV</a>, and Leo took advantage of a plot by several of its members to poison him, not only to inflict exemplary punishments by executing one (<a href="/wiki/Alfonso_Petrucci" title="Alfonso Petrucci">Alfonso Petrucci</a>) and imprisoning several others, but also to make radical changes in the college.<sup id="cite_ref-Chisholm1911_11-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Chisholm1911-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On 3 July 1517, he published the names of thirty-one new cardinals, a number almost unprecedented in the history of the <a href="/wiki/Papacy" class="mw-redirect" title="Papacy">papacy</a>. Among the nominations were such notable men such as <a href="/wiki/Lorenzo_Campeggio" title="Lorenzo Campeggio">Lorenzo Campeggio</a>, Giambattista Pallavicini, <a href="/wiki/Pope_Adrian_VI" title="Pope Adrian VI">Adrian of Utrecht</a>, <a href="/wiki/Thomas_Cajetan" title="Thomas Cajetan">Thomas Cajetan</a>, <a href="/wiki/Cristoforo_Numai" title="Cristoforo Numai">Cristoforo Numai</a> and <a href="/wiki/Egidio_Canisio" class="mw-redirect" title="Egidio Canisio">Egidio Canisio</a>. The naming of seven members of prominent Roman families, however, reversed the policy of his predecessor which had kept the political factions of the city out of the Curia. Other promotions were for political or family considerations or to secure money for the war against Urbino. The pope was accused of having exaggerated the conspiracy of the cardinals for purposes of financial gain, but most of such accusations appear unsubstantiated.<sup id="cite_ref-Chisholm1911_11-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Chisholm1911-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Leo, meanwhile, felt the need of staying the advance of the Ottoman <a href="/wiki/Sultan" title="Sultan">sultan</a>, <a href="/wiki/Selim_I" title="Selim I">Selim I</a>, who was threatening eastern <a href="/wiki/Europe" title="Europe">Europe</a>, and made elaborate plans for a crusade. A truce was to be proclaimed throughout Christendom; the pope was to be the arbiter of disputes; the emperor and the king of France were to lead the army; England, Spain and <a href="/wiki/Portugal" title="Portugal">Portugal</a> were to furnish the fleet; and the combined forces were to be directed against <a href="/wiki/Constantinople" title="Constantinople">Constantinople</a>. Papal <a href="/wiki/Diplomacy" title="Diplomacy">diplomacy</a> in the interests of peace failed, however; Cardinal Wolsey made England, not the pope, the arbiter between France and the Empire; and much of the money collected for the crusade from tithes and indulgences was spent in other ways.<sup id="cite_ref-Chisholm1911_11-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Chisholm1911-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1519 <a href="/wiki/Kingdom_of_Hungary" title="Kingdom of Hungary">Hungary</a> concluded a three years' truce with Selim I, but the succeeding sultan, <a href="/wiki/Suleiman_the_Magnificent" title="Suleiman the Magnificent">Suleiman the Magnificent</a>, renewed the war in June 1521 and on 28 August captured the citadel of <a href="/wiki/Siege_of_Belgrade_(1521)" title="Siege of Belgrade (1521)">Belgrade</a>. The pope was greatly alarmed, and although he was then involved in war with France he sent about 30,000 ducats to the Hungarians. Leo treated the <a href="/wiki/Eastern_Catholic" class="mw-redirect" title="Eastern Catholic">Eastern Catholic</a> Greeks with great loyalty, and by a bull of 18 May 1521 forbade <a href="/wiki/Latin" title="Latin">Latin</a> clergy to celebrate mass in Greek churches and Latin <a href="/wiki/Bishops" class="mw-redirect" title="Bishops">bishops</a> to ordain Greek clergy. These provisions were later strengthened by Clement VII and <a href="/wiki/Paul_III" class="mw-redirect" title="Paul III">Paul III</a> and went far to settle the constant disputes between the Latins and Uniate Greeks.<sup id="cite_ref-Chisholm1911_11-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Chisholm1911-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Protestant_Reformation">Protestant Reformation</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Leo_X&amp;action=edit&amp;section=7" title="Edit section: Protestant Reformation">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/History_of_Lutheranism#The_start_of_the_Reformation" title="History of Lutheranism">History of Lutheranism §&#160;The start of the Reformation</a></div>
<p>Leo was disturbed throughout his pontificate by schism, especially the <a href="/wiki/Protestant_Reformation" class="mw-redirect" title="Protestant Reformation">Reformation</a> sparked by <a href="/wiki/Martin_Luther" title="Martin Luther">Martin Luther</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Chisholm1911_11-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Chisholm1911-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Bulla-contra-errores.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/55/Bulla-contra-errores.jpg/250px-Bulla-contra-errores.jpg" decoding="async" width="250" height="341" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/55/Bulla-contra-errores.jpg/375px-Bulla-contra-errores.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/55/Bulla-contra-errores.jpg/500px-Bulla-contra-errores.jpg 2x" data-file-width="587" data-file-height="800" /></a><figcaption>Bulla <i>Contra errores Martini Lutheri</i> of 1521</figcaption></figure>
<p>In response to concerns about misconduct from some indulgence preachers, in 1517 <a href="/wiki/Martin_Luther" title="Martin Luther">Martin Luther</a> wrote his <i><a href="/wiki/Ninety-five_Theses" title="Ninety-five Theses">Ninety-five Theses</a></i> on the topic of indulgences. The resulting pamphlet spread Luther's ideas throughout Germany and Europe. Leo failed to fully comprehend the importance of the movement, and in February 1518 he directed the vicar-general of the <a href="/wiki/Augustinians" title="Augustinians">Augustinians</a> to impose silence on his <a href="/wiki/Monk" title="Monk">monks</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Chisholm1911_11-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Chisholm1911-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On 24 May, Luther sent an explanation of his theses to the pope; on 7 August he was summoned to appear at Rome. An arrangement was effected, however, whereby that summons was cancelled, and Luther went instead to <a href="/wiki/Augsburg" title="Augsburg">Augsburg</a> in October 1518 to meet the papal legate, <a href="/wiki/Cardinal_Cajetan" class="mw-redirect" title="Cardinal Cajetan">Cardinal Cajetan</a>; but neither the arguments of the cardinal, nor Leo's dogmatic papal bull of 9 November requiring all Christians to believe in the pope's power to grant indulgences, moved Luther to retract. A year of fruitless negotiations followed, during which the controversy took popular root across the German states.<sup id="cite_ref-Chisholm1911_11-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Chisholm1911-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>A further papal bull of 15 June 1520, <i><a href="/wiki/Exsurge_Domine" title="Exsurge Domine">Exsurge Domine</a></i> or <i>Arise, O Lord</i>, condemned forty-one propositions extracted from Luther's teachings, and was taken to Germany by Eck in his capacity as apostolic <a href="/wiki/Nuncio" title="Nuncio">nuncio</a>. Leo followed by formally excommunicating Luther by the bull <i><a href="/wiki/Decet_Romanum_Pontificem" title="Decet Romanum Pontificem">Decet Romanum Pontificem</a></i> or <i>It Befits the Roman Pontiff</i>, on 3 January 1521. In a brief, the Pope also directed <a href="/wiki/Charles_V,_Holy_Roman_Emperor" title="Charles V, Holy Roman Emperor">Charles V, Holy Roman Emperor</a> to take energetic measures against heresy.<sup id="cite_ref-Chisholm1911_11-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Chisholm1911-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Leo was pope during the <a href="/wiki/Lutheranism#Spread_into_northern_Europe" title="Lutheranism">spread of Lutheranism into Scandinavia</a>. The pope had repeatedly used the rich northern benefices to reward members of the Roman curia, and towards the close of the year 1516, he sent the impolitic Arcimboldi as papal nuncio to <a href="/wiki/Denmark" title="Denmark">Denmark</a> to collect money for St Peter's. This led to the <a href="/wiki/Reformation_in_Denmark-Norway_and_Holstein#Reformation_in_Denmark" class="mw-redirect" title="Reformation in Denmark-Norway and Holstein">Reformation in Denmark</a>. King <a href="/wiki/Christian_II_of_Denmark" title="Christian II of Denmark">Christian II</a> took advantage of the growing dissatisfaction of the native clergy toward the papal government, and of Arcimboldi's interference in the Swedish revolt, to expel the nuncio and summon Lutheran theologians to <a href="/wiki/Copenhagen" title="Copenhagen">Copenhagen</a> in 1520. Christian approved a plan by which a formal state church should be established in Denmark, all appeals to Rome should be abolished, and the king and diet should have final jurisdiction in ecclesiastical causes. Leo sent a new nuncio to Copenhagen (1521) in the person of the Minorite Francesco de Potentia, who readily absolved the king and received the <a href="/wiki/Diocese_of_Skara" title="Diocese of Skara">bishopric of Skara</a>. The pope or his legate, however, took no steps to correct abuses or otherwise discipline the Scandinavian churches.<sup id="cite_ref-Chisholm1911_11-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Chisholm1911-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Ara_Coeli_(12).JPG" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/12/Ara_Coeli_%2812%29.JPG/220px-Ara_Coeli_%2812%29.JPG" decoding="async" width="220" height="293" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/12/Ara_Coeli_%2812%29.JPG/330px-Ara_Coeli_%2812%29.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/12/Ara_Coeli_%2812%29.JPG/440px-Ara_Coeli_%2812%29.JPG 2x" data-file-width="1728" data-file-height="2304" /></a><figcaption>Statue of Leo X in the church of Santa Maria in Aracoeli, Rome</figcaption></figure>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Other_activities">Other activities</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Leo_X&amp;action=edit&amp;section=8" title="Edit section: Other activities">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Consistories">Consistories</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Leo_X&amp;action=edit&amp;section=9" title="Edit section: Consistories">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Cardinals_created_by_Leo_X" title="Cardinals created by Leo X">Cardinals created by Leo X</a></div>
<p>The pope created 42 new cardinals in eight consistories including two cousins (one who would become his successor Pope Clement VII) and a nephew. He also elevated <a href="/wiki/Pope_Adrian_VI" title="Pope Adrian VI">Adriaan Florensz Boeyens</a> into the cardinalate who would become his immediate successor Pope Adrian VI. Leo X's consistory of 1 July 1517 saw 31 cardinals created, and this remained the largest allocation of cardinals in one consistory until <a href="/wiki/Pope_John_Paul_II" title="Pope John Paul II">Pope John Paul II</a> named 42 cardinals in 2001.
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Canonizations">Canonizations</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Leo_X&amp;action=edit&amp;section=10" title="Edit section: Canonizations">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>Pope Leo X canonized eleven individuals during his reign with seven of those being a group cause of martyrs. The most notable canonization from his papacy was that of <a href="/wiki/Francis_of_Paola" title="Francis of Paola">Francis of Paola</a> on 1 May 1519.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Final_years">Final years</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Leo_X&amp;action=edit&amp;section=11" title="Edit section: Final years">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>That Leo did not do more to check the anti-papal rebellion in Germany and Scandinavia is to be partially explained by the political complications of the time, and by his own preoccupation with papal and Medicean politics in Italy. The death of the <a href="/wiki/Maximilian_I,_Holy_Roman_Emperor" title="Maximilian I, Holy Roman Emperor">emperor Maximilian</a> in 1519 had seriously affected the situation. Leo vacillated between the powerful candidates for the succession, allowing it to appear at first that he favoured Francis or a minor German prince. He finally accepted <a href="/wiki/Charles_V,_Holy_Roman_Emperor" title="Charles V, Holy Roman Emperor">Charles of Spain</a> as inevitable.<sup id="cite_ref-Chisholm1911_11-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Chisholm1911-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Leo was now eager to unite Ferrara, Parma and Piacenza to the States of the Church (The Papal States). An attempt late in 1519 to seize Ferrara failed, and the pope recognized the need for foreign aid. In May 1521 a treaty of alliance was signed at Rome between him and the emperor. Milan and <a href="/wiki/Genoa" title="Genoa">Genoa</a> were to be taken from France and restored to the Empire, and Parma and Piacenza were to be given to the Church on the expulsion of the French. The expense of enlisting 10,000 Swiss was to be borne equally by Pope and emperor. Charles V took Florence and the Medici family under his protection and promised to punish all enemies of the Catholic faith. Leo agreed to invest Charles V with the Kingdom of Naples, to crown him Holy Roman Emperor, and to aid in a war against Venice. It was provided that England and the Swiss might also join the league. Henry VIII announced his adherence in August 1521. Francis I had already begun war with Charles V in <a href="/wiki/Navarre" title="Navarre">Navarre</a>, and in Italy, too, the French made the first hostile movement on 23 June 1521. Leo at once announced that he would excommunicate the king of France and release his subjects from their allegiance unless Francis I laid down his arms and surrendered Parma and Piacenza to the Church. The pope lived to hear the joyful news of the capture of Milan from the French and of the occupation by papal troops of the long-coveted provinces (November 1521).<sup id="cite_ref-Chisholm1911_11-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Chisholm1911-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Having fallen ill with <a href="/wiki/Bronchopneumonia" title="Bronchopneumonia">bronchopneumonia</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup> Pope Leo X died on 1 December 1521, so suddenly that the last sacraments could not be administered; but the contemporary suspicions of poison were unfounded. He was buried in <a href="/wiki/Santa_Maria_sopra_Minerva" title="Santa Maria sopra Minerva">Santa Maria sopra Minerva</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Chisholm1911_11-13" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Chisholm1911-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Character.2C_interests_and_talents"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Character,_interests_and_talents">Character, interests and talents</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Leo_X&amp;action=edit&amp;section=12" title="Edit section: Character, interests and talents">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="General_assessment">General assessment</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Leo_X&amp;action=edit&amp;section=13" title="Edit section: General assessment">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Leo has been criticized for his handling of the events of the papacy.<sup id="cite_ref-13" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-13">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup> He had a musical and pleasant voice and a cheerful temper.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPastor190872,_74_14-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPastor190872,_74-14">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup> He was eloquent in speech and elegant in his manners and epistolary style.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPastor190878_15-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPastor190878-15">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup> He enjoyed music and the theatre, art and poetry, the masterpieces of the ancients and the creations of his contemporaries, especially those seasoned with wit and learning. He especially delighted in <a href="/wiki/Ex_tempore" title="Ex tempore">ex tempore</a> <a href="/wiki/Latin" title="Latin">Latin</a> verse-making (at which he excelled) and cultivated <i>improvisatori</i>.<sup id="cite_ref-16" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-16">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup> He is said to have stated, "Let us enjoy the papacy since God has given it to us",<sup id="cite_ref-17" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-17">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Ludwig_von_Pastor" title="Ludwig von Pastor">Ludwig von Pastor</a> says that it is by no means certain that he made the remark; and historian Klemens Löffler says that "the Venetian ambassador who related this of him was not unbiased, nor was he in Rome at the time."<sup id="cite_ref-18" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-18">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup> However, there is no doubt that he was by nature pleasure-loving and that the anecdote reflects his casual attitude to the high and solemn office to which he had been called.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPastor190876_19-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPastor190876-19">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup> On the other hand, in spite of his worldliness, Leo prayed, fasted, went to confession before celebrating Mass in public, and conscientiously participated in the religious services of the Church.<sup id="cite_ref-20" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> To the virtues of liberality, charity, and clemency he added the Machiavellian qualities of deception and shrewdness, so highly esteemed by the princes of his time.<sup id="cite_ref-Chisholm1911_11-14" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Chisholm1911-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-21" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-21">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The character of Leo X was formerly assailed by lurid aspersions of debauchery, murder, impiety, and atheism. In the 17th century it was estimated that 300 or 400 writers, more or less, reported (on the authority of a single polemical anti-Catholic source) a story that when someone had quoted to Leo a passage from one of the <a href="/wiki/Four_Evangelists" title="Four Evangelists">Four Evangelists</a>, he had replied that it was common knowledge "how profitable that fable of Christe hath ben to us and our companie".<sup id="cite_ref-22" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-22">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup> These aspersions and more were examined by <a href="/wiki/William_Roscoe" title="William Roscoe">William Roscoe</a> in the 19th century (and again by <a href="/wiki/Ludwig_von_Pastor" title="Ludwig von Pastor">Ludwig von Pastor</a> in the 20th) and rejected.<sup id="cite_ref-23" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-23">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup> Nevertheless, even the eminent philosopher <a href="/wiki/David_Hume" title="David Hume">David Hume</a>, while claiming that Leo was too intelligent to believe in Catholic doctrine, conceded that he was "one of the most illustrious princes that ever sat on the papal throne. Humane, beneficent, generous, affable; the patron of every art, and friend of every virtue".<sup id="cite_ref-24" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-24">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Martin_Luther" title="Martin Luther">Martin Luther</a>, in a <a href="https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/Letter_of_Martin_Luther_to_Pope_Leo_X" class="extiw" title="s:Letter of Martin Luther to Pope Leo X">conciliatory letter</a> to Leo, himself testified to Leo's universal reputation for morality:
</p>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r996844942">.mw-parser-output .templatequote{overflow:hidden;margin:1em 0;padding:0 40px}.mw-parser-output .templatequote .templatequotecite{line-height:1.5em;text-align:left;padding-left:1.6em;margin-top:0}</style><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>Indeed, the published opinion of so many great men and the repute of your blameless life are too widely famed and too much reverenced throughout the world to be assailed by any man, of however great name, or by any arts. I am not so foolish to attack one whom everybody praises ...<sup id="cite_ref-25" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-25">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>The final report of the Venetian ambassador <a href="/w/index.php?title=Marino_Giorgi&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Marino Giorgi (page does not exist)">Marino Giorgi</a> supports Hume's assessment of affability and testifies to the range of Leo's talents.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPastor190875ff_26-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPastor190875ff-26">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup> Bearing the date of March 1517 it indicates some of his predominant characteristics:<sup id="cite_ref-Chisholm1911_11-15" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Chisholm1911-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r996844942"><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>The pope is a good-natured and extremely free-hearted man, who avoids every difficult situation and above all wants peace; he would not undertake a war himself unless his own personal interests were involved; he loves learning; of canon law and literature he possesses remarkable knowledge; he is, moreover, a very excellent musician.<sup id="cite_ref-Chisholm1911_11-16" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Chisholm1911-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup> </p></blockquote>
<p>Leo is the fifth of the six popes who are unfavourably profiled by historian <a href="/wiki/Barbara_Tuchman" class="mw-redirect" title="Barbara Tuchman">Barbara Tuchman</a> in <i><a href="/wiki/The_March_of_Folly" title="The March of Folly">The March of Folly</a></i>, and who are accused by her of precipitating the <a href="/wiki/Protestant_Reformation" class="mw-redirect" title="Protestant Reformation">Protestant Reformation</a>. Tuchman describes Leo as a cultured – if religiously devout – <a href="/wiki/Hedonism" title="Hedonism">hedonist</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-27" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-27">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-28" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-28">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Intellectual_interests">Intellectual interests</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Leo_X&amp;action=edit&amp;section=14" title="Edit section: Intellectual interests">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Leo_X_Rubens.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/20/Leo_X_Rubens.jpg/237px-Leo_X_Rubens.jpg" decoding="async" width="237" height="302" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/20/Leo_X_Rubens.jpg/355px-Leo_X_Rubens.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/20/Leo_X_Rubens.jpg/474px-Leo_X_Rubens.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2518" data-file-height="3210" /></a><figcaption>17th century portrait of Leo X by <a href="/wiki/Peter_Paul_Rubens" title="Peter Paul Rubens">Peter Paul Rubens</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>Leo X's love for all forms of art stemmed from the humanistic education he received in <a href="/wiki/Florence" title="Florence">Florence</a>, his studies in <a href="/wiki/Pisa" title="Pisa">Pisa</a> and his extensive travel throughout Europe when a youth. He loved the Latin poems of the humanists, the tragedies of the Greeks and the comedies of <a href="/wiki/Bernardo_Dovizi" title="Bernardo Dovizi">Cardinal Bibbiena</a> and <a href="/wiki/Ludovico_Ariosto" title="Ludovico Ariosto">Ariosto</a>, while relishing the accounts sent back by the explorers of the <a href="/wiki/New_World" title="New World">New World</a>. Yet "Such a humanistic interest was itself religious. ... In the <a href="/wiki/Renaissance" title="Renaissance">Renaissance</a>, the vines of the classical world and the Christian world, of Rome, were seen as intertwined. It was a historically minded culture where artists' representations of <a href="/wiki/Cupid" title="Cupid">Cupid</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Mary_(mother_of_Jesus)" class="mw-redirect" title="Mary (mother of Jesus)">Madonna</a>, of <a href="/wiki/Hercules" title="Hercules">Hercules</a> and <a href="/wiki/St._Peter" class="mw-redirect" title="St. Peter">St. Peter</a> could exist side-by-side".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECrocker_III2001222_29-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECrocker_III2001222-29">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Love_of_music">Love of music</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Leo_X&amp;action=edit&amp;section=15" title="Edit section: Love of music">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Pastor says that "From his youth, Leo, who had a fine ear and a melodious voice, loved music to the pitch of fanaticism".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPastor1908144_30-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPastor1908144-30">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup> As pope he procured the services of professional singers, instrumentalists and composers from as far away as France, Germany and Spain. Next to goldsmiths, the highest salaries recorded in the papal accounts are those paid to musicians, who also received largesse from Leo's private purse. Their services were retained not so much for the delectation of Leo and his guests at private social functions as for the enhancement of religious services on which the pope placed great store. The standard of singing of the papal choir was a particular object of Leo's concern, with French, Dutch, Spanish and Italian singers being retained. Large sums of money were also spent on the acquisition of highly ornamented musical instruments, and he was especially assiduous in securing musical scores from Florence.<sup id="cite_ref-31" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-31">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup> He also fostered technical improvements developed for the diffusion of such scores. <a href="/wiki/Ottaviano_Petrucci" title="Ottaviano Petrucci">Ottaviano Petrucci</a>, who had overcome practical difficulties in the way of using movable type to print musical notation, obtained from Leo X the exclusive privilege of printing organ scores (which, according to the papal brief, "adds greatly to the dignity of divine worship") for a period for 15 years from 22 October 1513.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECummings1884–1885103ff_32-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECummings1884–1885103ff-32">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup> In addition to fostering the performance of sung Masses, he promoted the singing of the Gospel in Greek in his private chapel.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECummings2009586_33-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECummings2009586-33">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Unworthy_pursuits">Unworthy pursuits</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Leo_X&amp;action=edit&amp;section=16" title="Edit section: Unworthy pursuits">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Hanno.raffael.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e2/Hanno.raffael.jpg/250px-Hanno.raffael.jpg" decoding="async" width="250" height="245" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e2/Hanno.raffael.jpg/375px-Hanno.raffael.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e2/Hanno.raffael.jpg/500px-Hanno.raffael.jpg 2x" data-file-width="571" data-file-height="560" /></a><figcaption>Leo X's pet elephant, <a href="/wiki/Hanno_(elephant)" title="Hanno (elephant)">Hanno</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>Even those who defend him against the more outlandish attacks on his character acknowledge that he partook of entertainment such as masquerades, "jests," fowling, and hunting boar and other wild beasts.<sup id="cite_ref-34" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-34">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup> According to one biographer, he was "engrossed in idle and selfish amusements".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEVaughan1908283_35-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEVaughan1908283-35">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Leo indulged buffoons at his Court, but also tolerated behaviour which made them the object of ridicule. One case concerned the conceited <i>improvisatore</i> Giacomo Baraballo, Abbot of <a href="/wiki/Gaeta" title="Gaeta">Gaeta</a>, who was the butt of a burlesque procession organised in the style of an ancient <a href="/wiki/Roman_triumph" title="Roman triumph">Roman triumph</a>. Baraballo was dressed in festal robes of velvet and with ermine and presented to the pope. He was then taken to the piazza of St Peter's and was mounted on the back of <a href="/wiki/Hanno_(elephant)" title="Hanno (elephant)">Hanno, a white elephant</a>, the gift of King <a href="/wiki/Manuel_I_of_Portugal" title="Manuel I of Portugal">Manuel I of Portugal</a>. The magnificently ornamented animal was then led off in the direction of the <a href="/wiki/Capitoline_Hill" title="Capitoline Hill">Capitol</a> to the sound of drums and trumpets. But while crossing the <a href="/wiki/Ponte_Sant%27Angelo" title="Ponte Sant&#39;Angelo">bridge of Sant'Angelo</a> over the <a href="/wiki/Tiber" title="Tiber">Tiber</a>, the elephant, already distressed by the noise and confusion around him, shied violently, throwing his passenger onto the muddy riverbank below.<sup id="cite_ref-36" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-36">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Sexuality">Sexuality</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Leo_X&amp;action=edit&amp;section=17" title="Edit section: Sexuality">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Leo's most recent biographer, <a href="/wiki/Carlo_Falconi" title="Carlo Falconi">Carlo Falconi</a>, says Leo hid a private life of moral irregularity behind a mask of urbanity.<sup id="cite_ref-37" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-37">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup> Scabrous verse libels of the type known as <a href="/wiki/Pasquinade" title="Pasquinade">pasquinades</a> were particularly abundant during the conclave which followed Leo's death in 1521 and made imputations about Leo's unchastity, implying or asserting <a href="/wiki/Homosexuality" title="Homosexuality">homosexuality</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-38" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-38">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup> Suggestions of homosexual attraction appear in works by two contemporary historians, <a href="/wiki/Francesco_Guicciardini" title="Francesco Guicciardini">Francesco Guicciardini</a> and <a href="/wiki/Paolo_Giovio" title="Paolo Giovio">Paolo Giovio</a>. Zimmerman notes Giovio's "disapproval of the pope's familiar banter with his chamberlains – handsome young men from noble families – and the advantage he was said to take of them."<sup id="cite_ref-39" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-39">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Luther spent a month in Rome in 1510, three years before Leo became pontiff, and was disillusioned at the corruption he found there.<sup id="cite_ref-40" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-40">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> In 1520, the year before his excommunication from the Catholic Church, Luther claimed that Leo lived a "blameless life."<sup id="cite_ref-lutherleo_41-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-lutherleo-41">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup> However, Luther later distanced himself from this claim and alleged in 1531 that Leo had vetoed a measure that cardinals should restrict the number of boys they kept for their pleasure, "otherwise it would have been spread throughout the world how openly and shamelessly the pope and the cardinals in Rome practice sodomy."<sup id="cite_ref-lutherleo_41-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-lutherleo-41">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup> Against this allegation is the papal bull <i>Supernae dispositionis arbitrio</i> from 1514 which, <i>inter alia</i>, required cardinals to live "... soberly, chastely, and piously, abstaining not only from evil but also from every appearance of evil" and a contemporary and eye-witness at Leo's Court (Matteo Herculaneo), emphasized his belief that Leo was chaste all his life.<sup id="cite_ref-42" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-42">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Historians have dealt with the issue of Leo's sexuality at least since the late 18th century, and few have given credence to the imputations made against him in his later years and decades following his death, or else have at least regarded them as unworthy of notice; without necessarily reaching conclusions on whether he was homosexual.<sup id="cite_ref-43" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-43">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup> Those who stand outside this consensus generally fall short of concluding with certainty that Leo was unchaste during his pontificate.<sup id="cite_ref-44" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-44">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Joseph_McCabe" title="Joseph McCabe">Joseph McCabe</a> accused Pastor of untruthfulness and Vaughan of lying in the course of their treatment of the evidence, pointing out that Giovio and Guicciardini seemed to share the belief that Leo engaged in "unnatural vice" (homosexuality) while pope.<sup id="cite_ref-45" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-45">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Benevolence">Benevolence</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Leo_X&amp;action=edit&amp;section=18" title="Edit section: Benevolence">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Leo X made charitable donations of more than 6,000 ducats annually to retirement homes, hospitals, convents, discharged soldiers, pilgrims, poor students, exiles, cripples, and the sick and unfortunate.<sup id="cite_ref-46" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-46">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Death_and_legacy">Death and legacy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Leo_X&amp;action=edit&amp;section=19" title="Edit section: Death and legacy">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Death">Death</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Leo_X&amp;action=edit&amp;section=20" title="Edit section: Death">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Pope Leo X died fairly suddenly of pneumonia at the age of 45 on 1 December 1521 and was buried in Santa Maria sopra Minerva in Rome.<sup id="cite_ref-Pope_Leo_X_47-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Pope_Leo_X-47">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup> His death came just 10 months after he had excommunicated <a href="/wiki/Martin_Luther" title="Martin Luther">Martin Luther</a>, the seminal figure in the Protestant Reformation, who was accused of 41 errors in his teachings.<sup id="cite_ref-Pope_Leo_X_47-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Pope_Leo_X-47">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Failure_to_stem_the_Reformation">Failure to stem the Reformation</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Leo_X&amp;action=edit&amp;section=21" title="Edit section: Failure to stem the Reformation">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Possibly the most lasting legacy of the reign of Pope Leo X was his perceived failure not just to stem the Reformation but to fuel it.<sup id="cite_ref-Pope_Leo_X_47-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Pope_Leo_X-47">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup> A key issue was that his pontificate failed to bring about the reforms decreed by the <a href="/wiki/Fifth_Council_of_the_Lateran" title="Fifth Council of the Lateran">Fifth Lateran Council</a> (held between 1512 and 1517) which aimed to deal with many of their political problems as well as to reform Christendom, specifically relating to the papacy, cardinals, and curia. Some believe enforcing these decrees may have been enough to dampen support for radical challenges to church authority. But instead under his leadership, Rome's fiscal and political problems were deepened. A major contributor was his lavish spending (especially on the arts and himself) which led the papal treasury into mounting debt and his decision to authorize the sale of indulgences. The exploitation of people and corruption of religious principles linked to the practice of selling indulgences quickly became the key stimulus for the onset of the Protestant Reformation. Martin Luther's <i><a href="/wiki/95_Theses" class="mw-redirect" title="95 Theses">95 Theses</a></i>, otherwise entitled "Disputation on the Power and Efficacy of Indulgences", was posted on a Church door in Wittenberg, Germany in October 1517 just seven months after the Lateran V was completed. But Pope Leo X's attempt to prosecute Luther's teaching on indulgences, and to eventually excommunicate him in January 1521, did not get rid of Lutheran doctrine but had the opposite effect of further splintering the Western church.<sup id="cite_ref-Pope_Leo_X_47-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Pope_Leo_X-47">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Excessive_spending">Excessive spending</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Leo_X&amp;action=edit&amp;section=22" title="Edit section: Excessive spending">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Leo was renowned for spending money lavishly on the arts; on charities; on benefices for his friends, relatives, and even people he barely knew; on dynastic wars, such as the <a href="/wiki/War_of_Urbino" title="War of Urbino">War of Urbino</a>; and on his own personal luxury. Within two years of becoming Pope, Leo X spent all of the treasure amassed by the previous Pope, the frugal Julius II, and drove the Papacy into deep debt. By the end of his pontificate in 1521, the papal treasury was 400,000 ducats in debt.<sup id="cite_ref-Pope_Leo_X_47-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Pope_Leo_X-47">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup> This debt contributed not only to the calamities of Leo's own pontificate (particularly the sale of indulgences that precipitated <a href="/wiki/Protestantism" title="Protestantism">Protestantism</a>) but severely constrained later pontificates (Pope <a href="/wiki/Adrian_VI" class="mw-redirect" title="Adrian VI">Adrian VI</a>; and Leo's beloved cousin, <a href="/wiki/Clement_VII" class="mw-redirect" title="Clement VII">Clement VII</a>) and forced austerity measures.<sup id="cite_ref-48" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-48">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Leo X's personal spending was likewise vast. For 1517, his personal income is recorded as 580,000 ducats, of which 420,000 came from the states of the Church, 100,000 from <a href="/wiki/Annates" title="Annates">annates</a>, and 60,000 from the composition tax instituted by Sixtus IV.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (July 2023)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> These sums, together with the considerable amounts accruing from indulgences, jubilees, and special fees, vanished as quickly as they were received. To remain financially solvent, the Pope resorted to desperate measures: instructing his cousin, <a href="/wiki/Cardinal_Giulio_de%27_Medici" class="mw-redirect" title="Cardinal Giulio de&#39; Medici">Cardinal Giulio de' Medici</a>, to pawn the Papal jewels; palace furniture; tableware; and even statues of the apostles. Additionally, Leo sold cardinals' hats; memberships to a fraternal order he invented in 1520, the <i>Papal Knights of St. Peter and St. Paul</i>; and borrowed such immense sums from bankers that upon his death, many were ruined.<sup id="cite_ref-49" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-49">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>At Leo's death, the Venetian ambassador Gradenigo estimated the number of the Church's paying offices at 2,150, with a capital value of approximately 3,000,000 ducats and a yearly income of 328,000 ducats.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (December 2021)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Patron_of_learning">Patron of learning</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Leo_X&amp;action=edit&amp;section=23" title="Edit section: Patron of learning">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Leo X raised the Church to a high rank as the friend of whatever seemed to extend knowledge or to refine and embellish life. He made the capital of <a href="/wiki/Christendom" title="Christendom">Christendom</a>, Rome, a centre of <a href="/wiki/European_culture" class="mw-redirect" title="European culture">European culture</a>. While yet a cardinal, he had restored the church of Santa Maria in Domnica after Raphael's designs; and as pope he had <a href="/wiki/San_Giovanni_dei_Fiorentini" title="San Giovanni dei Fiorentini">San Giovanni dei Fiorentini</a>, on the <a href="/wiki/Via_Giulia" title="Via Giulia">Via Giulia</a>, built, after designs by <a href="/wiki/Jacopo_Sansovino" title="Jacopo Sansovino">Jacopo Sansovino</a><sup id="cite_ref-50" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-50">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup> and pressed forward the work on St Peter's Basilica and the Vatican under <a href="/wiki/Raphael" title="Raphael">Raphael</a> and <a href="/wiki/Agostino_Chigi" title="Agostino Chigi">Agostino Chigi</a>. Leo's constitution of 5 November 1513 reformed the Roman university, which had been neglected by Julius II. He restored all its faculties, gave larger salaries to the professors, and summoned distinguished teachers from afar;<sup id="cite_ref-51" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-51">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup> and, although it never attained to the importance of <a href="/wiki/Padua" title="Padua">Padua</a> or Bologna, it nevertheless possessed in 1514 a faculty (with a good reputation) of eighty-eight professors.
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Portrait_of_Pope_Leo_X.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f0/Portrait_of_Pope_Leo_X.jpg/220px-Portrait_of_Pope_Leo_X.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="298" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f0/Portrait_of_Pope_Leo_X.jpg/330px-Portrait_of_Pope_Leo_X.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f0/Portrait_of_Pope_Leo_X.jpg/440px-Portrait_of_Pope_Leo_X.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2000" data-file-height="2708" /></a><figcaption>Portrait of Pope Leo X (1846)</figcaption></figure>
<p>Leo called <a href="/wiki/Janus_Lascaris" title="Janus Lascaris">Janus Lascaris</a> to Rome to give instruction in Greek, and established a Greek printing press from which the first Greek book printed in Rome appeared in 1515. He made Raphael custodian of the classical antiquities of Rome and the vicinity, the ancient monuments of which formed the subject of a famous letter from Raphael to the pope in 1519.<sup id="cite_ref-52" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-52">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup> The distinguished Latinists <a href="/wiki/Pietro_Bembo" title="Pietro Bembo">Pietro Bembo</a> and <a href="/wiki/Jacopo_Sadoleto" title="Jacopo Sadoleto">Jacopo Sadoleto</a> were papal secretaries,<sup id="cite_ref-53" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-53">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup> as well as the famous poet <a href="/wiki/Bernardo_Accolti" title="Bernardo Accolti">Bernardo Accolti</a>. Other poets, such as <a href="/wiki/Marco_Girolamo_Vida" title="Marco Girolamo Vida">Marco Girolamo Vida</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-54" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-54">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Gian_Giorgio_Trissino" title="Gian Giorgio Trissino">Gian Giorgio Trissino</a><sup id="cite_ref-55" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-55">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup> and Bibbiena, writers of <i>novelle</i> like <a href="/wiki/Matteo_Bandello" title="Matteo Bandello">Matteo Bandello</a>, and a hundred other <i>literati</i> of the time were bishops, or papal scriptors or <a href="/wiki/Abbreviators" class="mw-redirect" title="Abbreviators">abbreviators</a>, or in other papal employs.
</p>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r996844942"><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>Under his pontificate, Latin Christianity assumed a pagan, Greco-Roman character, which, passing from art into manners, gives to this epoch a strange complexion. Crimes for the moment disappeared, to give place to vices; but to charming vices, vices in good taste, such as those indulged in by <a href="/wiki/Alcibiades" title="Alcibiades">Alcibiades</a> and sung by <a href="/wiki/Catullus" title="Catullus">Catullus</a>. —<a href="/wiki/Alexandre_Dumas" title="Alexandre Dumas">Alexandre Dumas <i>père</i></a><sup id="cite_ref-56" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-56">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Statesman">Statesman</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Leo_X&amp;action=edit&amp;section=24" title="Edit section: Statesman">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Several minor events of Leo's pontificate are worthy of mention. He was particularly friendly with King <a href="/wiki/Manuel_I_of_Portugal" title="Manuel I of Portugal">Manuel I of Portugal</a> as a result of the latter's missionary enterprises in <a href="/wiki/Asia" title="Asia">Asia</a> and <a href="/wiki/Africa" title="Africa">Africa</a>. Pope Leo X was granted a large embassy from the Portuguese king furnished with goods from Manuel's colonies.<sup id="cite_ref-57" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-57">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup> His concordat with Florence (1516) guaranteed the free election of the clergy in that city.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (January 2022)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>His constitution of 1 March 1519 condemned the King of Spain's claim to refuse the publication of papal bulls. He maintained close relations with <a href="/wiki/Poland" title="Poland">Poland</a> because of the Turkish advance and the Polish contest with the Teutonic Knights. His bull of July 1519, which regulated the discipline of the Polish Church, was later transformed into a concordat by <a href="/wiki/Clement_VII" class="mw-redirect" title="Clement VII">Clement VII</a>.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (January 2022)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>Leo showed special favours to the <a href="/wiki/Jews" title="Jews">Jews</a> and permitted them to erect a Hebrew <a href="/wiki/Printing" title="Printing">printing</a>-press at Rome. Under <a href="/wiki/Daniel_Bomberg" title="Daniel Bomberg">Daniel Bomberg</a>, that press produced manuscripts of the <a href="/wiki/Talmud" title="Talmud">Talmud</a><sup id="cite_ref-:6_58-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:6-58">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup> and <a href="/wiki/Mikraot_Gedolot" title="Mikraot Gedolot">Mikraot Gedolot</a> with Leo's approval and protection.<sup id="cite_ref-:0_59-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-59">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>He approved the formation of the Oratory of Divine Love, a group of pious men in Rome which later became the <a href="/wiki/Theatines" title="Theatines">Theatine Order</a>, and he canonized <a href="/wiki/Francis_of_Paola" title="Francis of Paola">Francis of Paola</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-60" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-60">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup>
</p>

<li id="cite_note-16"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-16">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Roscoe gives an instance of Leo's skill (<a href="#CITEREFRoscoe1806">Roscoe 1806</a>, p.&#160;493). <li id="cite_note-39"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-39">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><a href="/wiki/Paolo_Giovio" title="Paolo Giovio">Paolo Giovio</a>, <i>De Vita Leonis Decimi Pont. Max.</i>, Firenze (1548, 4 vols), written for the Medici Pope Clement VII and completed in 1533; and (covering the years 1492 to 1534) <a href="/wiki/Francesco_Guicciardini" title="Francesco Guicciardini">Francesco Guicciardini</a>, <i>Storia d'Italia</i>, Firenze (1561, first 16 books; 1564 full edn. 20 books) written between 1537 and 1540, and published after his death in the latter year. For the characterisation of the relevant passages (few and brief) in these authors, see, <i>e.g.</i>, <a href="#CITEREFVaughan1908">Vaughan 1908</a>, p.&#160;280: and Wyatt, Michael, "Bibbiena's Closet: Interpretation and the Sexual Culture of a Renaissance Papal Court", comprising chap. 2 of Cestaro, Gary P. (ed.), <i>Queer Italia</i>, London (2004) pp. 35–54 a.  To these can be added Zimmerman, T.P., <i>Paolo Giovio: The Historian and the Crisis of Sixteenth-Century Italy</i>, Princeton University Press (1996), citing at p. 23 Giovio's disapproval of the banter. Two pages later Zimmerman notes Giovio's <i>penchant</i> for gossip.</span>

<li id="cite_note-lutherleo-41"><span class="mw-cite-backlink">^ <a href="#cite_ref-lutherleo_41-0"><sup><i><b>a</b></i></sup></a> <a href="#cite_ref-lutherleo_41-1"><sup><i><b>b</b></i></sup></a></span> <span class="reference-text"><a href="#CITEREFWilson2007">Wilson 2007</a>, p.&#160;282;  This allegation (made in the pamphlet <i>Warnunge D. Martini Luther/ An seine lieben Deudschen</i>, Wittenberg, 1531) is in stark contrast to Luther's earlier praise of Leo's "blameless life" in a conciliatory letter of his to the pope dated 6 September 1520 and published as a preface to his <i>Freedom of a Christian</i>. See on this, <a href="#CITEREFHillerbrand2007">Hillerbrand 2007</a>, p.&#160;53.</span>

<li id="cite_note-43"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-43">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Those who have rejected the evidence include: Fabroni, Angelo, <i>Leone X: Pontificis Maximi Vita</i>, Pisa (1797) at p. 165 with note 84; <a href="#CITEREFRoscoe1806">Roscoe 1806</a>, pp.&#160;478–486; and (<a href="#CITEREFPastor1908">Pastor 1908</a>, pp.&#160;80f. with a long footnote). Those who have treated of the life of Leo at any length and ignored the imputations, or summarily dismissed them, include: <a href="/wiki/Ferdinand_Gregorovius" title="Ferdinand Gregorovius">Gregorovius, Ferdinand</a>, <i>History of the City of Rome in the Middle Ages</i> Eng. trans. Hamilton, Annie, London (1902, vol. VIII.1), p. 243; <a href="#CITEREFVaughan1908">Vaughan 1908</a>, p.&#160;280; <a href="/wiki/Carlton_J._H._Hayes" title="Carlton J. H. Hayes">Hayes, Carlton Huntley</a>, article "Leo X" in <i>The Encyclopædia Britannica</i>, Cambridge (1911, vol. XVI); <a href="/wiki/Mandell_Creighton" title="Mandell Creighton">Creighton, Mandell</a>, <i>A History of the Papacy from the Great Schism to the Sack of Rome</i>, London (new edn., 1919), vol. 6, p. 210; Pellegrini, Marco, articles "Leone X" in <i>Enciclopedia dei Papi</i>, (2000, vol.3) and <i><a href="/wiki/Dizionario_Biografico_degli_Italiani" title="Dizionario Biografico degli Italiani">Dizionario Biografico degli Italiani</a></i> (2005, vol. 64); and <a href="/wiki/Paul_Strathern" title="Paul Strathern">Strathern, Paul</a> <i>The Medici: Godfathers of the Renaissance</i> (a popular history), London (2003, pbk 2005), p. 277. Of these, <a href="/wiki/Ludwig_von_Pastor" title="Ludwig von Pastor">Ludwig von Pastor</a> and Hayes are known Catholics, and Roscoe, Gregorovius, and Creighton are known non-Catholics.</span>

<li id="cite_note-44"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-44">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">The most recent biography of the pope speculates that his private life may have been marked by moral irregularity: <a href="/wiki/Carlo_Falconi" title="Carlo Falconi">Falconi, Carlo</a>, <i>Leone X</i>, Milano (1987). <a href="/w/index.php?title=Giovanni_Dall%27Orto&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Giovanni Dall&#39;Orto (page does not exist)">Giovanni Dall'Orto</a> gathered and reviewed the most relevant material (including Falconi, pp. 455–461) in an entry in Wotherspoon &amp; Aldrich, <i>Who's Who in Gay and Lesbian History</i>, Routledge, London and New York (2001), at p. 264, arriving only at tentative and provisional conclusions as to Leo's suggested homosexuality.</span>
